# ESSAY
## ON THE NATURE OF COMMERCE IN GENERAL.

---

### *PART ONE.*

---

#### CHAPTER ONE.

->*Concerning Wealth.*<-

The Earth is the source or the matter from which Wealth is drawn; the labor of Man is the form which produces it <note>Cantillon uses the Aristotelian categories of "matter" and "form" to explain production—land provides the raw material, while human effort gives that material its useful shape.</note>: and Wealth in itself is nothing other <note>The sentence ends abruptly here due to the page break; in the original 1755 edition, it continues on the next page: "...than the maintenance, the conveniences, and the superfluities of life."</note>

<vocab>Wealth, Earth, Labor, Commerce, Nature</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter One of Cantillon's treatise, defining wealth as the product of land (the source) and labor (the active force). This distinction became a cornerstone for later economic theories, including those of the Physiocrats and Adam Smith.</summary>
<keywords>definition of wealth, factors of production, land and labor, 18th-century economics, Richard Cantillon</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter One, completing the definition of wealth started on page 1. It elaborates on the specific products of the earth and the role of labor in transforming natural resources into usable goods.</meta>

<header>Essay on Nature</header>

than sustenance, the conveniences, and the comforts <note>original: "agrémens." In 18th-century economic thought, this refers to things that make life pleasant or luxurious, often translated as "superfluities."</note> of life.

The Earth produces grass, roots, grains, flax, cotton, hemp, shrubs and woods of several species, along with fruits, bark, and foliage of various kinds, such as those of Mulberry trees for silkworms; it produces mines and minerals. Human labor <note>Cantillon continues his use of the "form" and "matter" metaphor; while the Earth provides the matter, labor is the active force that shapes it into "wealth."</note> gives the form of wealth to all of these.

Rivers and Seas provide fish for human sustenance, and many other things for enjoyment. But these Seas and Rivers belong to the adjacent lands, or are held in common; and human labor extracts the fish and other benefits from them.

# CHAPTER II.

## *On Human Societies.*

Regardless of the manner in which a Society of Men is formed, the ownership <note>Cantillon begins to argue that private property is not just a legal choice, but an inevitable social necessity for any organized group.</note> of the Land they inhabit will necessarily belong to a small number of them.

In nomadic societies—such as the Hordes of the Tartars <note>original: "Hardes des Tartares." Cantillon refers to the nomadic tribes of Central Asia; the term "Hardes" here is an archaic variant of "Hordes."</note> and the Camps of the Indians <note>original: "Indiens." In the 18th century, "Indians" (referring to Indigenous Americans) were frequently used as philosophical examples of societies living in a "state of nature" or as nomads.</note> who move from one place to another with their Livestock and Families—it is necessary for the Captain or the King who leads them to regulate the boundaries for each Head of Family, and the quarters for everyone around the camp. Otherwise, there would always be disputes over the quarters or conveniences—the wood, the grass, the water, etc. But once they have

<summary>In this opening of Chapter Two, Cantillon argues that land ownership naturally and necessarily concentrates in the hands of a few, regardless of whether a society is settled or nomadic.</summary>
<keywords>human society, land ownership, nomadic tribes, Tartars, Indigenous peoples, property rights, social organization</keywords>

<header>Essay on the Nature [of Commerce]</header>

In more regular Societies: If a **Prince** at the head of an **Army** has conquered a **Country**, he will distribute the Lands to his **Officers** or **Favorites**, according to their merit or his own good pleasure (which is the case where **France** originally began); he will establish **laws** to preserve the ownership for them and their **Descendants**: or else he will reserve the ownership of the **Lands** for himself, and employ his **Officers** or **Favorites** in the task of making them productive; or he will grant them to them on condition of paying a certain annual *cens* <note>original: "cens." This was a fixed, usually small, annual fee paid by a tenant to a lord, acknowledging the lord's ultimate ownership of the land.</note>, or *redevance* <note>original: "redevance." A general term for dues, rents, or royalties paid in exchange for the use of land or a right.</note>; or he will grant them while reserving the liberty to **tax** them every year, according to his needs and their means. In all these cases, these **Officers** or **Favorites**, whether they are absolute Owners, or

<summary>Cantillon describes how land ownership is established in settled societies, typically through conquest and distribution by a sovereign, using early France as a primary example.</summary>

If the **Prince** <note>In 18th-century political writing, "Prince" refers generally to any sovereign or ruler of a state.</note> makes the distribution of the **Lands** in equal portions to all the **Inhabitants**, they will nonetheless eventually fall into the possession of a small number. One **Inhabitant** will have several **Children**, and will not be able to leave to each of them a portion of **Land** equal to his own: another will die without **Children**, and will leave his portion to one who already has some, rather than to one who has none: a third will be lazy, extravagant, or sickly, and will find himself forced to sell his portion to another who possesses **frugality** and **industry**, who will continually increase his **Lands** through new purchases, for which he will employ the labor of those, who

### Essay on the Nature of Commerce

In the first settlement of **Rome**, each **Inhabitant** was given two **Journaux** <term>Journal: An old French unit of land measurement (similar to the Roman 'jugerum') representing the amount of land a man could plow or work in a single day.</term> of land; yet this did not prevent there being, shortly thereafter, an inequality in **estates** as great as that which we see today in all the States of **Europe**. The **Lands** fell into the possession of a small number of people.

Supposing then that the **Lands** of a new state belong to a small number of persons, each **Owner** will manage his Lands with his own hands, or will lease them to one or several **Farmers**. In this **economy**, it is necessary that the **Farmers** and **Laborers** find their **subsistence**; this is an indispensable necessity, whether the Lands are managed for the

The surplus of the product of the **Land** is placed at the disposal of the **Owner**; the latter gives a portion of it to the orders of the **Prince** or the **State**, or else the **Farmer** will give this portion directly to the **Prince**, deducting it from the rent due to the **Owner**.

As for the use to which the land should be put, it is a prerequisite to employ a part of it for the maintenance and nourishment of those who work upon it and make it productive: the remainder depends principally on the humors <note>original: "humeurs"; in the 18th century, this referred to one's temperament, inclinations, or personal whims.</note> and the way of life of the **Prince**, the **Lords of the State**, and the **Owner**; if they love drink, **Vineyards** must be cultivated; if they love silks, **Mulberry trees** must be planted and **silkworms** raised; and furthermore, a proportionate part of the land must be employed to main-

However, if one supposes that the **Lands** belong to no one in particular, it is not easy to conceive how a society of **Men** could be formed there: we see in the common **Lands**, for example, of a **Village**, that the number of **Cattle** <note>original: "Bestiaux"; a general term for livestock.</note> that each of the **Inhabitants** has the liberty to send there is regulated; and if one left the **Lands** to the first person who occupied them in a new conquest, or discovery of a **Country**, it would always be necessary to return to a rule to fix the **ownership** thereof, in order to be able to establish a **Society** of **Men** there, whether force or **Policy** <term>Policy: Translated from 'Police,' which in the 18th century referred to civil administration, internal regulation, and the maintenance of public order rather than just modern law enforcement.</term> decided this rule.

## CHAPTER III.

->*Of Villages.*<-

Whatever use is made of the **Land**, whether pasture, grain <note>original: "bled"; an archaic spelling of *blé*, meaning wheat or grain.</note>, or vineyards, it is necessary that the **Farmers** or **Laborers** <term>Laborers: translated from "Laboureurs," who in the 18th century were often higher-status tenant farmers who owned their own draft animals and plows, rather than simple landless peasants.</term>, who manage the work, reside very nearby; otherwise the time required to go to their **Fields** and return to their **Houses** would consume too great a portion of the day. Upon this point depends the necessity of **Villages** spread throughout all the **Countryside** and cultivated **Lands**, where one must also have **Blacksmiths** <note>original: "Maréchaux"; specifically those who shoe horses and repair iron tools.</note> and **Wheelwrights** <note>original: "Charons"; craftsmen who build and repair wooden wheels and wagons.</note> for the tools, the **Plow**, and the **Carts** that are needed; especially when the **Village** is distant from **Market Towns** and **Cities**. The size of a **Village** is naturally—

# CHAPTER IV.

## *Of Market Towns.*

THERE are some **Villages** where **Markets** have been established through the influence of some **Owner** or **Lord at Court** <note>original: "Seigneur en Cour"; refers to an aristocrat with influence in the royal household who could obtain legal privileges for their lands.</note>. These **Markets**, which are held once or twice a week, encourage several small **Entrepreneurs** <term>Entrepreneurs: In this historical context, the term refers to individuals who take the risk of buying goods at a certain price to resell them at an uncertain price.</term> & **Merchants** to settle in this place; where they buy at the **Market** the produce brought from the surrounding **Villages**, to transport and sell it in the **Cities**; they take in exchange from the **City** iron, salt, sugar, and other goods, which are sold on **Market** days to the **Inhabitants** of the **Villages**: one also sees several small **Artisans** settle in these places, such as **Locksmiths**, **Carpen-** <note>original: "Menui-"; the word is incomplete and likely finishes as "Menuisiers" (Carpenters) on the next page.</note>

<vocab>Villages, Markets, Owner, Lord, Entrepreneurs, Merchants, Cities, Inhabitants, Artisans, Locksmiths</vocab>

<summary>Cantillon explains the transition of a village into a market town, noting how the legal establishment of a market attracts merchants and artisans who act as intermediaries between rural producers and city consumers.</summary>
<keywords>market towns, trade, entrepreneurs, rural economy, artisans, commercial hubs, resource distribution</keywords>

[carpen]ters <note>original: "fiers"; this is the suffix of "charpentiers" (carpenters), continuing the list of artisans from the previous page.</note> and others, for the needs of the **Villagers** who have none in their own **Villages**, and finally these **Villages** become **Market Towns**.

A **Market Town** being situated as if in the center of the **Villages** whose **Inhabitants** come to the **Market**, it is more natural and easier for the **Villagers** to bring their **produce** there on **Market** days to sell it, and to buy there the **merchandise** they need, than to see these goods carried by the **Merchants** and **Entrepreneurs** into the **Villages** to receive there in exchange the **produce** of the **Villagers**.

1. The circuits of the **Merchants** through the **Villages** would increase the **expense** of **transport** <note>original: "Voitures"; in this context, it refers to the cost of carriage or transport of goods.</note> without necessity.

2. These **Merchants** would perhaps be obliged to go to several **Villages** before finding the quality and quantity of **produce** they wish to purchase.

4°. It would be almost impossible to fix the price of **produce** and **merchandise** in the **Villages** between these **Merchants** and the **Villagers**. The **Merchant** would refuse, in one **Village**, the price asked for the **produce**, in the hope of finding it at a better price <note>original: "à meilleur marché"; literally "at a better market" or cheaper.</note> in another **Village**, and the **Villager** would refuse the price that the **Merchant** offers for his **merchandise**, in the hope that another **Merchant** who comes along will take it at a better rate. <note>original: "à meilleur compte"; meaning on more favorable terms or more profitably for the seller.</note>

All these inconveniences are avoided when the **Villagers** come on **Market** days to the **Market Town** <note>original: "Bourg"; a large village or small town serving as a local administrative and commercial hub.</note>, to sell their **produce** there, and to buy the **merchandise** they [need]... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, to be continued on the following page.</note>

### CHAPTER V.

#### *Of Cities.*

The **Landowners** who have only small portions of **Land** usually live in the **Market Towns** and **Villages**, near their **Lands** and **Farmers**. The transport of the **produce** that comes to them into the **Cities**

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But if we set aside these ideas so as not to complicate our subject, it may be said that the gathering of several wealthy **Landowners** <note>original: "Propriétaires de Terres"</note>, who reside together in the same place, is sufficient to form what is called a **City**, and that many **Cities** in **Europe**, in the interior of the country, owe the number of their **Inhabitants** to this gathering. In this case, the size of a **City** is naturally proportioned to the number of **Landowners** who reside there, or rather to the product of the **Lands** belonging to them—after deducting the costs of **transport** for those whose **Lands** are the most distant, and the portion they are obliged to provide to the **King** or the **State**, which must ordinarily be consumed in the **Capital**.

# CHAPTER VI.

## On Capital Cities

A **Capital** is formed in the same manner as a **provincial Town**; with this difference: that the largest **Landowners** of the entire State reside in the Capital; that the King or the supreme Government makes its home there, and spends the **revenues of the State** there; that the **Courts of Justice** of last resort <note>The highest courts from which there is no further appeal.</note> reside there; that it is the center of **Fashions** <note>original: "Modes"</note> which all the Provinces take as a model; and that the **Landowners** who reside in the Provinces do not fail to come sometimes to spend some time in the Capital, and to send their Children there to be polished <note>original: "façonner," referring to the social education and refinement expected of the upper classes.</note>. Thus all the Lands

<term>Capitale: Capital</term>, <term>Ville de province: Provincial town</term>, <term>Propriétaires des Terres: Landowners</term>, <term>Gouvernement: Government</term>, <term>revenus de l'Etat: State revenues</term>, <term>Cours de Justice: Courts of Justice</term>, <term>Modes: Fashions/Trends</term>

<summary>This page defines a capital city as being distinguished from provincial towns by the presence of the central government, the highest courts, and the residence of the wealthiest landowners, which establishes the city as the economic and cultural center of the state.</summary>
<keywords>Capital city, urbanization, Landowners, State revenue, central government, social refinement, economic center</keywords>

We have a very recent example of this in the City of St. Petersburg, to the detriment of Moscow <note>Peter the Great moved the Russian capital from Moscow to the newly built St. Petersburg in 1712 to "open a window to the West."</note>; and one sees many ancient Cities, which were once significant, fall into ruin, while others are reborn from their debris. *The price and intrinsic value*

the other **Acre** <term>Arpent: a pre-metric French unit of land area, roughly equivalent to an acre.</term>, assuming the labor is the same; and the **wool** produced by the one will sell at the same price as that which is produced by the other.

If the **Wool** on one side is worked into a suit of coarse cloth, and the **Wool** on the other into a suit of fine cloth; as this latter suit will require a greater amount of labor, and a more expensive labor than that of the coarse cloth, it will sometimes be ten times more expensive, although both suits contain the same quantity of **Wool** and of the same quality <note>original: "même bonté," literally "same goodness."</note>. The quantity of the land's product, and the quantity as well as the quality of the labor, will necessarily enter into the price.

# Essay on the Nature
<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>of Commerce.</header>

the land, according to its quality.

The price of a pitcher of water from the river Seine is nothing, because it is an immense matter that never runs dry; but one pays a *sol* <note>sol: A small French coin of the period, also known as a *sou*; it represented a minor denomination in the pre-revolutionary French currency system.</note> for it in the streets of Paris, which is the price or the measure of the labor of the **Water-carrier**.

By these inductions and examples, I believe it will be understood that the price or the <term>intrinsic value</term> <note>intrinsic value: For Cantillon, this is the fundamental cost of production based on resources, distinct from the fluctuating market price.</note> of a thing is the measure of the quantity of land and of labor that enters into its production, having regard for the quality or product of the land, and for the quality of the labor.

But it often happens that many things which currently have this <term>intrinsic value</term> are not sold at Market according to this value: that will depend on the moods and fancies of men, and on the consumption they will make.

<vocab>intrinsic value, labor, land, production, market, consumption, Richard Cantillon</vocab>

<summary>Cantillon defines intrinsic value as the sum of land and labor required for production, using the Paris water-carrier to illustrate how even "free" resources acquire value through labor. He distinguishes this stable internal value from market prices, which fluctuate based on human desire and demand.</summary>

# Of Commerce. PART I.
<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>of Commerce. PART I.</header>

If a **Lord** <note>original: "Seigneur"; here meaning a wealthy landowner or member of the nobility.</note> digs canals and raises terraces in his Garden, the <term>intrinsic value</term> <note>intrinsic value: The fundamental cost of an item based on the resources (land) and effort (labor) used to create it, regardless of its eventual price.</note> of it will be proportionate to the land and the labor; but the sale price will not always follow this proportion: if he offers to sell this Garden, it may happen that no one will want to give him even half of the expense he incurred there; and it may also happen, if several people desire it, that he will be given double the **intrinsic value**, that is to say, the value of the land and the expense he has put into it.

If the **Farmers** in a State sow more grain <note>original: "blé"; while specifically "wheat," it was often used as a general term for all bread-grains in 18th-century economic writing.</note> than usual, that is to say, much more grain than is needed for the year's consumption, the <term>intrinsic</term> and real value of the grain will correspond to the land and the labor that go into its production: but as there is a

<summary>Cantillon illustrates the disconnect between production costs and market prices, explaining that while land and labor determine a "real" value, the final price is driven by buyer desire and supply levels. He uses the examples of a costly private garden and an overabundance of grain to show how market prices can swing above or below intrinsic value.</summary>
<keywords>intrinsic value, market price, supply and demand, grain production, Richard Cantillon, labor theory of value, land ownership</keywords>

### Essay on the Nature
<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>Essay on the Nature</header>

too great an abundance, and more Sellers than Buyers; the price of **grain** <note>original: "blé"; referring to wheat or bread-grains, the primary food staple of the era.</note> at the **Market** will necessarily fall below the price or <term>intrinsic value</term> <note>intrinsic value: The stable cost of production based on land and labor, distinct from the fluctuating market price.</note>. If, on the contrary, the **Farmers** sow less grain than is necessary for consumption, there will be more Buyers than Sellers, and the price of grain at the Market will rise above its **intrinsic value**.

There is never any variation in the **intrinsic value** of things; but the impossibility of proportioning the production of merchandise and commodities to their consumption in a **State** <note>meaning a nation or organized political community</note>, causes a daily variation, and a perpetual ebb and flow in **Market** prices. However, in well-regulated Societies, the **Market** prices of commodities and merchandise whose consumption is fairly constant and uniform, do not deviate

The **Earth** is the matter, and **labor** the form, of all commodities and merchandise; and since those who work must necessarily subsist on the product of the **Earth**, it seems that one could find a relationship between the value of labor and that of the product of the **Earth**: this will be the subject of the following Chapter.

## CHAPTER XI.
### Of the <term>Par</term> or Ratio of the Value of Land to the Value of Labor.

It does not appear that **Providence** <note>In this context, the author refers to the divine or natural order of the world.</note> has given the right of possession of **Land** to one Man rather than to another. The most ancient Titles are founded on violence and conquests. The Lands of **Mexico** belong today to the **Spaniards**, and those of **Jerusalem** to the **Turks**. But in whatever manner one attains the ownership and possession of Land, we have already remarked that it always falls to a small number of persons in proportion to all the inhabitants.

If a **Proprietor** of a large Estate <note>original: "Terre"; refers here to a significant landed property or estate.</note> undertakes to have it—

<note>The text ends mid-sentence as the author prepares to describe the management of land by an owner.</note>

<summary>Cantillon argues that land ownership is rooted in historical conquest rather than natural right, noting that land tends to concentrate in the hands of a few. This sets the stage for his investigation into the economic relationship between land and the labor required to work it.</summary>

**Doctor Halley** <note>original: "Docteur Halley." This refers to Edmond Halley (1656–1742), the English astronomer who also pioneered the field of actuarial science by creating the first life expectancy tables.</note>: thus, it is necessary to raise two Children to ensure one reaches the working age, and it would appear that this tally does not fully account for the replacement of labor, because adult Men die at every age.

This circumstance seems to decrease the expense required to raise a Child until the working age; but as Mothers lose much time in caring for their Children during their infirmities & infancy, and as even adult Girls do not equal the labor of Males, and barely earn enough to subsist;

<summary>Cantillon uses Edmond Halley’s mortality tables to argue that because half of all children die before age seventeen, the cost of labor must account for the expense of raising multiple children to replace a single worker. He notes that while high infant mortality reduces the total years of support needed, the time mothers lose to childcare and the lower wages of women offset these savings.</summary>

...following the ways of life in our **Europe**, where the **Peasants** of different countries are often fed and maintained quite differently.

This is why I have not determined exactly how much **Land** the labor of the lowliest **Peasant** or **Laborer** corresponds to in value, even though I have said that it is worth double the produce of the **Land** used to maintain him; for this varies according to the way of life in different countries. In some southern provinces of *France*, the **Peasant** supports himself on the produce of an **acre and a half** <term>Acre</term> <note>original: "arpent." A French land measure. Cantillon uses this to quantify the "land footprint" of a person's lifestyle.</note> of **Land**, and one can estimate his labor there as equal to the produce of three **acres**. # Essay on the Nature <note>The full title of this 1755 work is *Essai sur la Nature du Commerce en Général*.</note>

the **Inhabitants** do not plow the land, and where they live solely by hunting, the lowliest **Hunter** can consume the product of 50 **acres** <term>Arpent</term> <note>original: "arpens." An old French unit of area. # Essay on Commerce.

These individuals <note>Referring to the transport entrepreneurs mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> undertake to pay the Farmer a fixed price for his produce—the market price of the day—in order to fetch an uncertain price in the **City**. # on Trade. PART I. 71

...an infinity of others that could be made on a subject that concerns all the **Inhabitants** of a **State**, it can be established that, except for the **Prince** and the **Landowners**, all the **Inhabitants** of a **State** are dependent; that they can be divided into two classes: namely **Entrepreneurs**, and **Wage-earners**; and that the **Entrepreneurs** are, as it were, on uncertain wages, while all the others are on certain wages for the time they enjoy them, even though their functions and their rank are very disproportionate. The **General** who has a salary, the **Courtier** <note>A person who attends the royal court; here, someone receiving a royal allowance or pension.</note> who has a pension, and the **Servant** who has wages, fall under this latter category. All the others are **Entrepreneurs**, whether they set themselves up with capital <note>original: "un fond" — the initial money, stock, or resources required to start a business.</note> to conduct their enterprise, or whether they are **Entrepreneurs** of their own labor without any capital, and they can...

<summary>Cantillon defines a revolutionary economic framework where everyone except the sovereign and landowners is classified as either an "entrepreneur" facing risk or a "wage-earner" with a fixed income. Notably, he places high-ranking generals and lowly servants in the same category because both receive a guaranteed payment.</summary>

<keywords>economic classes, entrepreneurs, wage-earners, social hierarchy, financial risk, capital, landownership, Richard Cantillon</keywords>

### On Commerce. Part I. 75

-nership <note>Completing the word "ownership" or "property" from the previous page.</note> of lands only concerns those who maintain possession of them; and as all lands always have a Master or current **Owner**, I always assume that it is from their resources <note>original: "fond" — referring to the productive base, capital, or soil of the land.</note> that all the **Inhabitants** of the State draw their subsistence and all their wealth. If these **Owners** all limited themselves to living on their rents, this would be beyond doubt, and in such a case, it would be much more difficult for the other Inhabitants to enrich themselves at their expense.

I shall therefore establish as a principle that the **Landowners** are the only ones who are naturally independent in a State; that all other classes are dependent, whether as **Entrepreneurs** <term>Entrepreneurs: in Cantillon’s theory, these are individuals who buy at a certain price to sell at an uncertain one, thus taking on risk</term> or as wage-earners, and that all exchange and circulation within the State is conducted through the mediation of these **Entrepreneurs**.

->D ij<-

<summary>Cantillon establishes a fundamental economic principle: landowners are the only truly independent members of society, while everyone else—including "entrepreneurs" and wage-earners—is dependent on the land's output. He identifies the entrepreneur as the key agent who drives the circulation of goods and wealth.</summary>

### Essay on the Nature [of Trade]

For if some of the **Farmers** sowed on their **Farm** or portion of land more grain than usual, they will have to feed a smaller number of **Sheep**, and they will have less wool and less mutton to sell; consequently, there will be too much grain and too little wool for the consumption of the **Inhabitants**. There will therefore be a high price <note>original: "cherté"</note> for wool, which will force the **Inhabitants** to wear their clothes longer than usual; and there will be low prices <note>original: "grand marché," meaning a "great market" or bargain</note> for grain and a surplus for the following year. And as we suppose that the **Owner** has stipulated in money the payment of one-third of the **Farm's** produce, which must be paid to him, the **Farmers** who have too much wheat and too little wool will not be in a position to pay him his rent. ## Essay on the Nature <note>The full title of the work is *Essai sur la Nature du Commerce en Général* or *Essay on the Nature of Trade in General*.</note>

in the present example, we have assumed that all the **Inhabitants** live in roughly the same way as they did when the **Proprietor** managed his land himself; consequently, the **Farmers** will use the land for the same purposes as before.

The **Proprietor**, who has one-third of the **land's product** <note>Cantillon famously theorized that the produce of the land is divided into three "rents": one for the landowner, one for the farmer's expenses, and one for the farmer's profit.</note> at his disposal, is the primary actor in any variations that may occur in **consumption**. The **Laborers** and **Artisans** who live from day to day change their way of living only through necessity; if there are some **Farmers**, **Master Artisans**, or other well-off **Entrepreneurs** <note>original: "Entrepreneurs." Cantillon is credited with being the first to define the entrepreneur as a risk-taker who buys at a certain price to sell at an uncertain one.</note> who vary their spending and **consumption**, they always take the **Lords** and **Proprietors** of the land as their model. They imitate them in their clothing, in their cuisine, and in their

<summary>Cantillon argues that landowners are the primary drivers of economic demand, as their consumption choices set the trends that other social classes, such as entrepreneurs and artisans, inevitably imitate.</summary>

->of Trade. PART I.<-

way of living. If the **Landowners** <note>original: "Propriétaires." In Cantillon's framework, Landowners are the only truly independent actors because they own the source of all production.</note> take pleasure in wearing fine linen, silks, or lace, the consumption of these goods will be greater than the consumption that the **Landowners** [personally] engage in.

If a **Lord**, or **Landowner**, who has leased all his **Lands** to be farmed <note>original: "donné toutes ses Terres à ferme." This refers to the system where a farmer pays a fixed rent to the owner to manage the land.</note>, takes a whim to notably change his way of living; if, for example, he decreases the number of his **Domestic Servants** and increases that of his **Horses**; not only will his **Servants** be forced to leave the **Land** in question, but also a proportionate number of **Artisans** and **Laborers** who worked to provide for their maintenance: the portion of land that was used to maintain these **Inhabitants** will instead be used as **Pasture** <note>original: "Prairies."</note> for the additional **Horses**, and if all the **Landowners** of a **State** did the same;

# CHAPTER XV.

*The multiplication and the decrease of the Population <note>original: "Peuples." In this 18th-century context, it refers to the total number of inhabitants or the common people within a nation.</note> in a State depend principally on the will, the fashions, and the ways of living of the Landowners.*

Experience shows us that we can multiply Trees, Plants, and other kinds of vegetation, and that we can maintain as much of them as the portion of land destined for them can nourish.
The same experience shows us that we can likewise multiply all species of Animals, and maintain them in such quantity as the portion of land destined for them can nourish. If one establishes Stud farms <note>original: "Haras," which are specialized facilities for breeding horses.</note>, herds of Cattle, or flocks of Sheep,

<term>multiplication of peoples</term>: The growth of the population. <term>State</term>: A political entity or nation. <term>Landowners</term>: Those who hold title to the land and thus control its production. <term>vegetation</term>: All forms of plant life. <term>Animals</term>: Livestock and other creatures. <term>Stud farms</term>: Horse-breeding estates. <term>Cattle</term>: Bovine livestock. <term>Sheep</term>: Ovine livestock.

<summary>Cantillon argues that the population of a state is not fixed but fluctuates based on the choices of landowners. He posits that just as land can be used to grow specific amounts of plants or livestock, its capacity to support human life is dictated by how those in power choose to use the soil.</summary>
<keywords>population growth, land ownership, agricultural capacity, livestock, resource allocation, Richard Cantillon, demographics</keywords>

duction. The more horses are maintained in a State, the less subsistence <note>In 18th-century economics, "subsistence" refers to the total amount of food and resources available to support a human population.</note> will remain for the inhabitants. The maintenance of carriage, hunting, or parade horses often costs three to four acres <note term="arpens">An "arpent" is an old French measure of land area, roughly equivalent to an acre.</note> of land.

But when the Lords <note>original: "Seigneurs"</note> and Landowners obtain their cloth, silks, lace, etc., from foreign manufactures, and pay for them by sending the produce of the State's commodities abroad, they thereby extraordinarily diminish the subsistence of the inhabitants and increase that of foreigners, who often become the enemies of the State.

<summary>Cantillon argues that elite spending on luxury horses and foreign imports reduces the domestic food supply. # of Trade. PART I.

### Essay on the Nature <note>Full title: *Essai sur la Nature du Commerce en général* (Essay on the Nature of Trade in General)</note>

...will also pay to **Foreigners** in produce grown in **Poland**, there will be well over a full third of the product of the land in **Poland** that is taken away from the subsistence of the **Inhabitants**, and, what is worse, the greater part of which is sent abroad, and often serves for the maintenance of the **Enemies of the State**. <note>Cantillon is writing in a Mercantilist context, where the goal of trade was to strengthen one's own nation relative to rivals; exporting food to feed "enemies" was seen as a strategic disaster.</note> If the **Landowners** and the **Lords** in **Poland** wished to consume only the **Manufactures** of their own State, however poor they might be in their beginnings, they would make them become better little by little, and would maintain a great number of their own **Inhabitants** working there, instead of giving this advantage to **Foreigners**: and if all **States** took similar care not to be the dupes <note>By "dupes," Cantillon refers to nations that trade away essential raw materials and food for finished luxury goods, which he believes weakens the country's population and power.</note> of other **States** in **Commerce**, each **State** would be significant solely in proportion to its produce and

<summary>Cantillon concludes that when both elites and farmers prefer foreign luxuries, over a third of a nation's resources are exported, harming domestic growth. He argues that even "poor" domestic manufacturing is better for the State than importing, as it supports the local population.</summary>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If every Landowner, in a State, had only a small portion of land, similar to that which is usually left to the management of a single Farmer, there would be almost no Cities; and the Inhabitants would be more numerous and the State would be very rich, if each of these Landowners employed the Inhabitants fed by his land in some useful labor.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But when the Lords <term>Seigneurs: high-ranking nobles or landowners who held significant social and economic power</term> have vast landholdings, they inevitably bring about luxury and idleness. <header>### On Commerce. Part I. ###</header>

...draw from poor people, through charities, the subsistence which should instead sustain them in their labor. They cause them to lose much time in useless conversations; not to mention those who scheme within Families, and those who are vicious. Experience shows that the States which have embraced Protestantism, and which have neither Monks nor Mendicants <term>Mandians: members of religious orders who live by begging rather than through endowments or manual labor</term>, have visibly become more powerful because of it. They also enjoy the advantage of having suppressed a great number of Feast days <term>Fêtes: religious holidays on which labor was prohibited by the Church</term> which are observed in Roman Catholic countries, and which diminish the labor of the Inhabitants by nearly an eighth part of the year.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If one wished to make the most of everything in a State, one could, it seems to me, diminish the number of Mendicants there by incorporating them into the Monkeries <note>The original term "Moinerie" is a somewhat disparaging collective noun for monks and the monastic system</note>, as va- <note>The word is cut off at the end of the page; given the catchword "ls," it likely concludes as "vacancies" (vacancies or openings) on the following page.</note>

The real or intrinsic value <term>Valeur intrinseque: a foundational economic concept suggesting that an object's worth is based on the cost of the land and labor required to produce it, regardless of its market price</term> of Metals, as with all things, is proportioned to the land and the labor necessary for their production. # Essai sur la nature

that is to say, the labor of the Mine is often the primary expense, and often the ruin, of the **Entrepreneur** <note>In the context of this 18th-century text, the "entrepreneur" refers to someone who undertakes a venture—like operating a mine—paying fixed costs for labor and land in hopes of an uncertain profit</note>.

The market value of metals, as with all merchandise or commodities, is sometimes above and sometimes below the **intrinsic value** <term>Intrinsic value: defined by the author as the measure of the land and labor required for production</term>, and varies in proportion to their abundance or rarity, according to the consumption that is made of them.

If the Landowners, and the other lower orders of a State who imitate them, rejected the use of **tin** and **copper**—under the supposition, however false, that these metals are harmful to health—and if they universally used earthenware dishes and kitchenware <note>original: "batterie de terre"; refers to clay-based pots, pans, and utensils</note>, these metals would be sold at a very low price in the Markets, and the work conducted to extract them from the Mine would be discontinued.

But since these Metals are found to be useful, and are employed in the everyday needs of life, they will always have a value in the Market that corresponds to their abundance or rarity, and to the consumption made of them; and they will always be extracted from the Mine to replace the quantity that is lost <note>original: "périt"; literally "perishes," referring here to the physical degradation, wear-and-tear, or loss of metal over time</note> through daily use.

Iron is not only useful for the needs of common life, but one could say that it is, in a way, necessary; and if the Americans <note>original: "Amériquains"; refers to the indigenous peoples of the Americas prior to or during the early colonial period</note>, who did not use it before the discovery of their Continent, had discovered Mines and known its uses, there is no doubt that they would have worked toward the production of this metal, whatever the costs <note>original: "frais"; refers to the land and labor expenses mentioned in earlier chapters</note> it might have incurred.

...even for most of the uses made of lead and iron. They also have this advantage over other metals, that fire does not consume them, and they are so durable that they can be regarded as permanent bodies: it is therefore not surprising that Men, who have found other metals useful, should have valued gold and silver even before they were used in barter <note>original: "troc"; the direct exchange of goods before the invention of currency</note>. The Romans valued them from the foundation of Rome, and yet they did not use them for money <note>original: "monnoie"; specifically refers to minted coinage here</note> until five hundred years later. Perhaps all other Nations did the same, and only adopted these metals for money long after they had been used for other ordinary purposes. However, we find from the most ancient Historians that from time immemorial, gold and [silver] were used...

### On Commerce. Part I. 131

...silver as money in Egypt and in Asia; and we learn in Genesis <note>The first book of the Bible; the author is likely referring to Genesis 23, where Abraham purchases a burial plot for silver.</note> that silver money was being manufactured in the time of Abraham.

Suppose now that the first silver was found in a mine on Mount Niphates <note>A mountain range in the Armenian Highlands, often cited by ancient geographers as the source of the Tigris River in Mesopotamia.</note> in Mesopotamia. It is natural to believe that one or several Landowners, finding this metal beautiful and useful, first made use of it, and that they willingly encouraged the Miner or the <term>Entrepreneur</term> <note>In the context of this 18th-century text, an "Entrepreneur" is someone who undertakes a project or business at their own financial risk, a central concept in early economic theory.</note> to extract more from the mine, by giving him—in exchange for his labor and that of his assistants—as much produce of the land as was necessary for their maintenance.

As this metal became more and more esteemed in Mesopotamia, if the great Landowners bought silver ewers <note>original: "aiguieres"; these are decorative water pitchers or jugs, indicating the metal's use as a status symbol before it became a common currency.</note>, the lower orders, according to their means or savings,

<meta>This page continues the thought from page 131, where the author describes the transition of silver from a luxury material for the elite into a medium of exchange used by the general population.</meta>

...could buy silver goblets; and the <term>Entrepreneur</term> <note>In this 18th-century context, the "Entrepreneur" is the person managing the mine and bearing the financial risk.</note> of the Mine, seeing a constant demand for his merchandise, undoubtedly assigned it a value proportioned to its quality or weight in exchange for the other commodities or goods he received. While all the Inhabitants regarded this metal as something precious and durable, and strove to possess some pieces of it, the Entrepreneur—who alone was able to distribute it—was in some way able to demand an arbitrary quantity of other commodities and goods in exchange.

### ESSAY ON THE NATURE OF COMMERCE. PART I.

<header>Essay on the Nature</header>

Common usage has reserved the term **intrinsic value** <note>original: "valeur intrinſèque"</note> for gold and silver, to designate and signify the quantity of true gold or silver contained in a bar of metal; however, throughout this essay, I have consistently used the term **intrinsic value** to define the quantity of **land** and **labor** <note>Cantillon is famous for this "land-and-labor" theory of value, which argues that the "real" price of any product is determined by the amount of land needed to grow the raw materials and the amount of human work required to process them.</note> that enters into the production of things, having found no term more fitting to express my thought. Furthermore, I only provide this warning so that no one is misled; and when gold and silver are not the subject of discussion, the term will always be appropriate, without any ambiguity.

We have seen that metals, such as gold, silver, iron, etc., serve many uses, and that they have a **real value** proportioned to the land and labor that enter into their production. We shall see in the...

<vocab>intrinsic value, land, labor, production, metals, gold, silver, real value</vocab>

<summary>Cantillon defines his specific use of "intrinsic value" as the measure of land and labor required for production, distinguishing it from the metallurgical definition of metal purity. He asserts that all metals possess a real value based on these two fundamental economic factors.</summary>

<keywords>intrinsic value, land and labor, theory of value, production costs, economic definitions, precious metals, real value</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues Part I of the essay. Having established the "intrinsic value" of goods based on land and labor on the previous page, Cantillon now transitions to the practical necessity of a "common measure" or currency to facilitate the bartering of these goods.</meta>

<header>of Commerce. PART I.</header>

...second part of this essay, that Men were forced by necessity to use a **common measure** <note>original: "meſure commune"; Cantillon is discussing the economic origin of money as a standard to compare different goods.</note> to determine, during barter, the proportion and the **value** of the **provisions** <term>denrées: basic agricultural commodities and food supplies</term> and merchandise they wished to exchange. The only question is to see which commodity or merchandise is most suitable for this common measure, and whether it was necessity, rather than mere taste, that led to the preference for **gold**, **silver**, and **copper**, which are generally used for this purpose today.

Ordinary commodities, such as grains, wine, meat, etc., certainly have a **real value** and serve the needs of life; however, they are all perishable, and even inconvenient to transport, and are consequently ill-suited to serve as a common measure.

<summary>Cantillon argues that the necessity of trade forced humanity to adopt a "common measure" (money) to compare the value of different goods. He notes that while food and basic supplies have real value, their perishability and bulk make them unsuitable for use as currency compared to metals.</summary>

<keywords>common measure, money, barter, gold, silver, copper, commodities, real value, perishability, transport of goods</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 141, where Richard Cantillon began evaluating which commodities are fit to serve as money. After dismissing basic food supplies (provisions) due to their perishability and bulk, he now examines whether manufactured goods or gemstones could fulfill the role of a universal currency.</meta>

<header>*Essay on the Nature*</header>

Merchandise—that is to say, cloths, linens, leathers, etc.—are also perishable, and cannot be subdivided without changing their value for human use in some way; like basic provisions, they incur many transport costs; they even require expense for their storage: consequently, they are poorly suited to serve as a **common measure** <note>original: "meſure commune"; in this context, it refers to a standard unit of account or currency used to value all other goods.</note>.

Diamonds and other precious stones, even if they did not have an **intrinsic value** <term>valeur intrinſeque: for Cantillon, this is the amount of land and labor required to produce something, rather than its market price.</term>, and were valued only by taste, would be suitable to serve as a common measure if they were not recognizable <note>original: "reconnoiſſables"; Cantillon means that each stone is unique and distinguishable, making it impossible to have a standardized unit like a coin.</note> and if they could be subdivided without loss <note>original: "déchet"; referring to the loss of mass and value that occurs when a large gemstone is cut into smaller pieces.</note>. But with these defects, and the fact that they are not suited for general utility, they cannot serve as a common measure.

<vocab>merchandise, common measure, diamonds, precious stones, intrinsic value, expense, transport</vocab>

<summary>Cantillon explains why neither manufactured goods nor gemstones are suitable to act as money. Manufactured goods are bulky and perishable, while precious stones lack the uniformity and divisibility required for a standard currency.</summary>
<keywords>common measure, currency, intrinsic value, merchandise, precious stones, divisibility, transport costs, economic utility</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues Cantillon’s systematic evaluation of various materials to serve as money. <header>of Commerce. PART I.</header>

Iron, which is always useful and quite durable, would not serve poorly if more suitable alternatives were not available. It is consumed by fire; and due to the quantity required, it takes up too much volume. It was used from the time of **Lycurgus** <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta, credited with establishing the city's military-oriented social and political system.</note> until the **Peloponnesian War** <note>A major conflict between Athens and Sparta (431–404 BC) that reshaped the ancient Greek world.</note>. However, since its value was necessarily regulated by its **intrinsic value** <term>intrinsic value: for Cantillon, this is the cost of production measured in land and labor.</term>, or in proportion to the land and labor involved in its production, a great quantity was required for even a small value. What was bizarre was that they spoiled its quality with vinegar to make it unfit for ordinary human use, preserving it only for **barter** <note>original: "troc"; here used to describe the use of iron as a medium of exchange.</note>. Thus, it could only serve the austere people of **Sparta**, and could not persist even among them once they expanded their interactions with other countries. To ruin the Lace- <margin>Lacedaemonians</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 146 regarding the production costs of precious metals. It concludes the explanation of why silver is more valuable than copper by highlighting the human cost of mining, and begins a foundational argument about "intrinsic value" in currency.</meta>

### ->On Trade. Part I.<-

-tion. The reason for the high cost <note>original: "cherté"</note> of silver miners is that they hardly live more than five or six years in this labor, which causes a high mortality rate; in such a way that a small piece of silver corresponds to as much land and labor as a large piece of copper.

Money—or the common measure of values—must correspond, really and intrinsically, in terms of the price of land and labor, to the things given for it in barter <note>original: "troc"</note>. Without this, it would have only an imaginary value. For example, if a Prince or a Republic <note>In the 18th century, a "Republic" referred to any self-governing state, such as Venice or the Netherlands, rather than just a modern representative democracy.</note> authorized as currency within the State something that lacked such real and intrinsic value, not only would other States refuse to accept it on those terms, but the inhabitants themselves would reject it once they perceived—

The author is distinguishing between biological survival and social existence.</note>, could subsist without all this. But one must not conclude from this that all these things have only an imaginary value. They have one in proportion to the land and the labor that go into their production. Gold and silver, like other merchandise and like provisions <note>original: "denrées," referring to basic foodstuffs or raw commodities.</note>, can only be extracted with costs roughly proportionate to the value assigned to them; and whatever Men produce by their labor, this labor must provide for their maintenance <note>original: "entretien." This is a central concept in the author's economic theory: that the price of labor must at least cover the cost of the worker's survival and the continuation of their trade.</note>. This is the great principle that one hears every day even from the mouths of the common folk <note>original: "petites Gens," referring to the working classes or those of lower social standing.</note> who do not enter into

<summary>Cantillon argues that although many goods are not necessities, their value is not arbitrary; instead, it is determined by the "intrinsic value" of the land and labor required to produce them. He asserts that the market value of gold and silver must cover the costs of their extraction and the maintenance of the laborers involved.</summary>

of money, just as all the **Beef** put up for sale at the **Market** is to all the money brought there to buy **Beef**.

This proportion is settled through **bargaining** <note>original: "l'altercation." In this economic context, it refers to the process of "haggling" or the verbal exchange between supply and demand to find a price.</note>. The **Butcher** maintains his price based on the number of buyers he sees; the **Buyers**, for their part, offer less according to whether they believe the **Butcher** will have less turnover <note>original: "débit," referring to the rate of sale or volume of business.</note>. The price settled by a few is usually followed by the others. Some are more skilled at promoting their merchandise, while others are more adept at disparaging it. Although this method of fixing the prices of things at the **Market** has no just or geometrical foundation—since it often depends on the eagerness or the flexibility of a small number of **Buyers** or **Sellers**—nevertheless, it does not appear that one could achieve it by any other

If, instead of forty litrons, there happen to be four hundred, not only will the stewards <note>original: "Maîtres d’hôtels." It often happens that the **Sellers**, by wanting to maintain their price too high at the Market, miss the opportunity to sell their foodstuffs <term>denrées: raw agricultural products or basic provisions</term> or their **merchandise** advantageously, and that they lose thereby. It also happens that by maintaining these prices they may often sell more advantageously another day.
   Distant Markets can always influence the prices of the Market where one is: if wheat is extremely expensive in France, it will rise in England and in the other neighboring Countries.

However, when a <term>Farmer: In the 18th century, a "farmer" was a businessman who leased land from a landlord to run an agricultural enterprise, rather than a simple laborer.</term> has the funds to manage the operation of his Farm, the Landlord, who grants him his Farm for a third of the produce, will be certain of his payment; he will find himself better off with such a bargain than if he had leased his Farm at a higher price to a destitute Farmer, at the risk of losing all his rent. The larger the Farm, the more well-off the Farmer will be. This is seen in England, where Farmers are usually more prosperous than in other Countries where Farms are small.

### Essay on the Nature of Commerce. Part II. 176

...would probably not differ from the calculation I make for other **States** which subsist principally on their own resources, and which are the subject of this **Essay**.

Regarding **cash** <note>original: "argent comptant." This refers to liquid money or ready currency available for immediate transactions.</note> necessary to conduct **trade** with **Foreigners**, it seems that no more is needed than that which circulates within the **State**, when the **balance of trade** <note>original: "la balance du commerce." This is the difference in value between a country's exports and imports; Cantillon is describing a state of "equilibrium."</note> with the **Foreigner** is equal—that is to say, when the **commodities** and **merchandise** sent there are equal in value to those received from there.

If **France** sends **cloths** to **Holland**, and if she receives **spices** in return for the same value, the **proprietor** <note>The "proprietor" here refers to the land-owning class, whose consumption of luxury imports like spices drives international trade.</note> who consumes these spices pays their value to the **Grocer**, and the **Grocer** pays this same value to the **Cloth Manufacturer**, to whom the same value is owed in **Holland** for the cloth he has sent there.

<summary>Cantillon explains that in a balanced trade economy, where exports equal imports, no additional gold or silver needs to leave the country. # Essay on the Nature of Trade

...partly the **City** or whether it remains there entirely, it can be considered as constituting the **circulation** of the **City** & the **Countryside**. All **circulation** takes place between the **inhabitants** of the **State**, and all these **inhabitants** are fed and maintained in every way by the **produce of the lands** and the **growth** of the **countryside**.

It is true that wool, for example, which is drawn from the **Countryside**, is worth four times more than it was when it is made into cloth in the **City** <note>Cantillon is describing what modern economists call "value added" through manufacturing, though he frames it within a system where the ultimate source of all wealth remains the land.</note>. But this increase in value, which is the price of the labor of the **Workers** & **Manufacturers** of the **City**, is again exchanged for the **provisions** of the **Countryside** which serve to maintain these **Workers**.

<note>original: "MVSEVM BRITANNICVM" — British Museum library stamp</note>

**Vocabulary**
**circulation**, **City**, **Countryside**, **inhabitants**, **State**, **produce of the lands**, **growth**, **wool**, **cloth**, **Workers**, **Manufacturers**, **provisions**

<summary>Cantillon explains that all economic circulation occurs between urban and rural populations, asserting that every citizen is ultimately sustained by agricultural output. He notes that while manufacturing increases the value of raw materials like wool, that extra value is simply used by urban workers to purchase the food and resources grown in the countryside.</summary>

->on Trade. PART II. 183<-

# CHAPTER IV.

## *Further reflection on the speed or slowness of the circulation of money in exchange.*

Suppose that the **Farmer** pays 1300 ounces of silver each quarter to the **landowner**, and that the landowner distributes 100 ounces of this in small amounts every week to the **Baker**, the **Butcher**, etc., and that these **Entrepreneurs** <note>Cantillon is one of the first economic writers to define the "Entrepreneur" as someone who buys at a certain price to sell at an uncertain price, bearng risk.</note> cause these 100 ounces to return every week to the **Farmer**, in such a way that the **Farmer** collects as much money per week as the landowner spends. In this scenario, there will only be 100 ounces of silver in perpetual **circulation**, and the other 1200 ounces will remain in the **till** <note>original: "en caisse" — referring to money held in reserve or a cash box rather than actively moving through the economy.</note>, partly in the hands of the **landowner** and partly in the hands of the **Farmer**.

<term>Trade: The act of buying and selling goods and services.</term>
<term>Farmer: A tenant who manages land and pays rent to a proprietor.</term>
<term>Landowner: The proprietor who owns the land and receives rent.</term>
<term>Silver: Used here as the primary medium of exchange or money.</term>
<term>Circulation: The movement of money through the economy as it changes hands.</term>
<term>Exchange: The act of giving one thing and receiving another (original: "troc").</term>
<term>Entrepreneurs: Individuals who take financial risks to provide goods or services.</term>
<term>Ounces: A unit of weight for measuring silver currency.</term>
<term>Till: A reserve of cash not currently being spent.</term>

<summary>Cantillon uses a mathematical model to demonstrate how the velocity of money affects the total amount of currency needed in an economy. He shows that a small amount of silver (100 ounces) can sustain a much larger debt (1300 ounces) if it circulates quickly enough between the farmer, the landlord, and city merchants.</summary>

<keywords>velocity of money, monetary circulation, silver currency, cash reserves, economic modeling, Richard Cantillon, tenant farmers, landownership</keywords>

->Essay on Nature. PART II. 184<-

But it rarely happens that **landowners** spend their rents in a constant and regular proportion. In London, as soon as a **landowner** receives his rent, he puts the greater part of it into the hands of a **Goldsmith** <note>In the 18th century, London goldsmiths functioned as early bankers, accepting deposits and issuing notes.</note> or a **Banker**, who lend it out at interest; consequently, this portion circulates. Or else, this **landowner** spends a good portion of it on the purchase of various things necessary for the household; and before he can receive a second quarterly payment <note>original: "quartier" — refers to the standard practice of paying land rents four times a year.</note>, he will perhaps borrow money. Thus, the money from this first quarter will circulate in a thousand ways before it can be collected and returned to the hands of the **Farmer**, to be used to make the payment for the second quarter.

When the time for the payment of this second quarter arrives, the **Farmer** will sell his produce <note>original: "denrées" — agricultural commodities or foodstuffs intended for sale.</note> in large quantities; and those who

# CHAPTER V.

## *On the inequality of the circulation of actual money in a State.*

The **City** always provides many goods to the **Countryside**, and the <term>landowners</term> <note>original: "propriétaires de terres"</note> who reside in the **City** must always receive there about one-third of the produce of their lands; thus the **Countryside** owes the **City** more than half of the land's produce. This debt would always exceed half if all the owners resided in the **City**; but as many of the less significant ones live in the **Countryside**, I assume that the <term>balance</term>, <note>In this context, "balance" refers to the net difference in a trade account.</note> or the debt, which continually returns from the **Countryside** to the **City**, is equal to half of the produce

It would be easy to draw an infinite number of **inferences** <note>original: "inductions"; logical conclusions drawn from specific observations.</note> of the same nature, to justify by experience the necessity of an **inequality of the circulation of money** <note>The author argues that money does not distribute itself evenly, but concentrates where debts and taxes are owed.</note> in the different **Provinces** of a large **State** or **Kingdom**, and to demonstrate that this inequality is always relative to the **balance** <note>The net flow of payments; here, the debt the provinces owe the capital through taxes and rents.</note> or debt belonging to the **Capital**.

I estimate in general that an increase in effective money <term>argent effectif: the physical supply of gold and silver coins actually present and circulating within an economy</term> causes within a State a proportionate increase in consumption, which produces, by degrees, an increase in prices.

If the increase of effective money comes from gold or silver mines located within a State, the Owner of these mines, the Entrepreneurs, the Smelters, the Refiners, and generally all those who work there, will not fail to increase their expenses in proportion to their gains. They will consume in their households more meat and more wine or beer than they did before; they will become accustomed to wearing better clothes, finer linens, to having more decorated houses, and other more sought-after conveniences. Consequently, they

<note>The author, likely Richard Cantillon, is describing what is now known as the "Cantillon Effect." This theory suggests that the first recipients of new money (like mine owners) spend it first, driving up prices in certain sectors before the rest of the population receives the new currency.</note>

## CHAPTER VII.

### **Continuation of the same subject: the increase and decrease of the quantity of hard money in a State.**

Since gold, silver, and copper have an intrinsic value <term>intrinsic value: the fundamental cost of a commodity based on the land and labor required to produce it, independent of market fluctuations</term> proportioned to the land and labor that enter into their production at the places where they are extracted from the Mines, and additionally to the costs of their importation or introduction into States that have no Mines, the quantity of money—like that of all other merchandise—determines its value in the bargaining <note>original: "altercations"; refers to the process of haggling or price negotiation in a market</note> of the Markets against all other things.

If England begins for the first time to make use of gold, silver, and copper in

<margin>Opponents among whom money is scarcer.</margin>

If two Princes go to war for the Sovereignty or the Conquest of a State, and one has a great deal of money while the other has little, but possesses several domains that might be worth twice as much as all the money of his Enemy; the first will be in a better position to attach Generals and Officers to his cause through largesses in money, than the second will be by giving his own men double the value in lands and domains. Grants of land are subject to disputes and <term>rescissions</term> <note>From the French *rescisions*, referring to the legal voiding or peeling back of a contract or deed, making land grants less secure than cash.</note>, and one cannot count on them as reliably as on the money one receives. One can give money for secret services and without witnesses; whereas lands, produce, and goods—

<page-num>256</page-num>
->Essay on Nature<-

But the path of these loans, which provides a temporary advantage, leads to a bad end; it is but a <note>original: *feu de paille*—literally a "straw fire," meaning a brief flash that dies out quickly.</note> flash in the pan. To restore a State, one must focus on bringing in a real balance of trade <term>balance of trade: the difference in value between a country's imports and exports</term> annually and consistently. This is achieved by using shipping to help manufacturing and industry flourish, as these goods can always be sent to foreigners at a lower price when a nation has fallen into decay and suffers from a scarcity of <term>specie: money in the form of gold or silver coins</term> actual coin.

In such a recovery, the Merchants are the first to make their fortunes. Then, the <term>Nobility of the Robe: high-ranking legal and administrative officials who often purchased their titles</term> <note>original: *Gens de robbe*</note> are able to appropriate a portion of that wealth. Finally, the Prince and the <term>Tax Farmers: private financiers who contracted with the crown to collect taxes</term> <note>original: *Traitans*</note> can acquire wealth at the expense of both groups, distributing favors and graces as they see fit. However, when money becomes too abundant within the State, luxury will take root, and the nation will once again fall into decay.

<summary>The author argues that while loans provide short-term relief, true national recovery requires a positive balance of trade and industrial growth. He warns that the resulting cycle of wealth—moving from merchants to officials and the crown—eventually leads to excessive luxury and a new period of decline.</summary>
<keywords>loans, balance of trade, manufacturing, shipping, merchants, Nobility of the Robe, tax farmers, luxury, economic cycle</keywords>

## CHAPTER IX.

### *Of the interest of money, and its causes.*

Just as the prices of things are fixed in the altercations <note>In this 18th-century context, "altercations" refers to the active bargaining and haggling process that occurs between buyers and sellers to reach a market price.</note> of the markets by the quantities of things exposed for sale in proportion to the quantity of money offered for them—or what is the same thing, by the numerical proportion of Sellers and Buyers—similarly, the interest of money in a State is fixed by the numerical proportion of Lenders and Borrowers.

Although money serves as pledges <term>pledges: the author uses the word "gages," suggesting that money acts as a physical security or a token of value that facilitates the exchange of goods.</term> in barter, it nevertheless does not multiply at all, and produces no interest in simple circulation. The necessities of Men seem to have

<meta>catchword: introduced</meta>

<summary>The author explains that interest rates are not arbitrary but are determined by the market forces of supply and demand between lenders and borrowers, just as the prices of goods are set by sellers and buyers. He notes that money itself is a static medium of exchange that does not naturally "grow" or produce interest simply by circulating.</summary>
<keywords>interest rates, lenders, borrowers, market prices, supply and demand, circulation of money, barter, economic theory</keywords>

Paris sets himself up as an <term>Entrepreneur: Cantillon uses this term to describe anyone who conducts business at a risk for an uncertain profit, from large merchants to street vendors.</term> of his own labor; all the **capital** <term>capital: original "fond," meaning the initial investment or assets needed to start a business.</term> he will require will be the price of two buckets, which he can buy for one ounce of silver, after which everything he earns becomes profit. If he earns fifty ounces of silver a year through his labor, the ratio of his **capital**, or loan, to his profit will be as one to fifty. That is to say, he will earn five thousand percent, whereas the **Hatter** <term>Hatter: a skilled artisan whose business requires significant raw materials (like wool or fur) and tools, making his overhead much higher than a laborer's.</term> will not even earn fifty percent, and will even be obliged to pay twenty to thirty percent of that to the **Lender**.

However, a **Lender** of money would much rather lend a thousand ounces of silver to a single **Hatter** at twenty percent interest, than to lend a thousand ounces to a thousand **Water-carriers** at five hundred percent interest. The **Casuists** <term>Casuists: Theologians who study "cases of conscience," applying moral principles to specific, often complex, human situations to determine what is right or wrong.</term>, who hardly seem suited to judge the nature of interest and matters of commerce, have devised a term (**loss sustained** <note>original: "damnum emergens." A Latin legal and theological term. In the context of usury laws, it allowed a lender to charge interest as compensation for a specific loss they suffered because they did not have the money on hand.</note>) by means of which they are willing to tolerate these high interest rates; and rather than overturn the custom and convenience of **Societies**, they have consented and permitted those who lend with great risk to draw a proportionally high interest. They allow this without limits, for they would be hard-pressed to find specific ones, since the matter truly depends on the fears of the **Lenders** and the necessities of the **borrowers**.

It appears from these examples and by these inferences, that there are within a State several classes and pathways of interest or profit; that in the lowest classes, interest is always the highest in proportion to the greatest risk; and that it decreases from class to class until the highest, which is that of **Merchants** <term>Négocians: At this time, the term specifically referred to high-level wholesale merchants or international traders, rather than small-scale shopkeepers.</term> who are wealthy and reputed to be **solvent**. <term>Solvable: Having the assets and reliability to pay off all debts.</term> The interest stipulated in this class is what is called the **market rate** of interest in the State, and it differs little from the interest stipulated on the **mortgage** of lands. <summary>Cantillon explains that interest rates are not uniform but exist on a scale based on risk, with the lowest rates being the "market rate" offered to wealthy, reliable merchants. If in a State there were no **Entrepreneurs** <term>Entrepreneurs: A central figure in Cantillon's theory; anyone who operates at a risk, buying at a certain price to sell at an uncertain one.</term> who could make a **profit** on the money or on the goods they borrow, the use of **interest** would probably not be as frequent as we see. There would only be extravagant and profligate people who would take out loans.

But given the habit everyone has of employing Entrepreneurs, there is a constant source for loans and, consequently, for interest. It is the Entrepreneurs who cultivate the lands, the Entrepreneurs who provide the bread, the meat, the clothing, etc., to all the Inhabitants of a city. ### On Commerce. PART II.

[augmen]tation of the quantity of actual money in a State decreases the price of interest there, because when money is abundant, it is easier to find some to borrow. This idea is not always true or accurate. To be convinced of this, one need only remember that in the year 1720 <note>The author refers to the period of the South Sea Bubble in England and John Law's Mississippi Scheme in France, a time of intense financial speculation.</note>, almost all the money in England was brought to London; and beyond that, the number of notes <note>original: "billets," referring to paper bank notes or credit instruments used during the speculative mania.</note> placed on the market accelerated the movement of money in an extraordinary manner. However, this abundance of money and circulation, instead of decreasing the current interest—which was previously at five percent and below—only served to increase its price, which was driven up to fifty and sixty percent. It is easy to explain this increase in the price of interest by the principles and causes of the in-

<summary>The author challenges the prevailing economic theory that a higher money supply always lowers interest rates, using the English financial crisis of 1720 as a counter-example where rates actually skyrocketed.</summary>

<keywords>interest rates, money supply, 1720, London, bank notes, circulation, South Sea Bubble, inflation</keywords>

--- Page 288 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of interest rates during the 1720 financial crisis. The text begins mid-word, completing the explanation of why interest rates rose despite an increase in the money supply.</meta>

### Essay on the Nature of Commerce

[in]terest, which I established in the previous chapter. It is this: everyone had become an <term>Entrepreneur</term> <note>The author uses this term to describe anyone who takes a financial risk or undertakes a venture for profit, rather than just a modern business owner.</note> in the South Sea scheme, and sought to borrow money to buy <term>Shares</term> <note>original: "Actions."</note>, counting on making an immense profit by means of which they could easily pay this high rate of interest.

If the abundance of money in the State comes into the hands of people who lend, it will undoubtedly decrease the current interest rate by increasing the number of lenders; but if it comes through the mediation of people who spend, it will have the exact opposite effect. It will raise the price of interest by increasing the number of <term>Entrepreneurs</term> who find new work through this increase in spending, and who will need to borrow to fund their enterprises, across all classes of interest.

**Vocabulary:** South Sea, Shares, interest, Entrepreneurs, money, spending, system

<summary>The author explains that during the South Sea Bubble, interest rates rose because the high demand for speculative loans outpaced the money supply. He argues that interest rates only drop if new money enters the economy via lenders, whereas money entering via spenders actually drives rates up by increasing business demand.</summary>
