## of Commerce. PART II.

The abundance or scarcity of money in a State always raises or lowers the prices of all things in the negotiations of trade <note>original: "altercations du troc." This refers to the process of haggling and exchange in the marketplace.</note>, without having any necessary connection with the rate of interest, which may very well be high in States where money is abundant, and low in those where money is scarcer: high where everything is expensive, and low where everything is a bargain <note>original: "à grand marché."</note>: high in London, and low in Genoa.

The rate of interest rises and falls every day based on simple rumors, which tend to decrease or increase the security of the Lenders, without the price of goods in trade being altered for that reason.

The most constant source of high interest in a State is the great expenditure of the Lords <note>original: "Seigneurs."</note> and Landowners, or of other wealthy people.

The <term>Entrepreneurs</term> <note>As noted previously, this refers to individuals who undertake financial ventures or risks for profit.</note> and master Cra[ftsmen] <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "Artisans."</note>

<summary>The author argues that there is no direct correlation between the quantity of money (and resulting inflation) and interest rates, noting that interest is primarily driven by lender security and the spending habits of the wealthy.</summary>

<keywords>interest rates, money supply, inflation, London, Genoa, lenders, landowners, entrepreneurs, market security</keywords>

### Essay on the Nature [of Commerce]

[Ar]tisans <note>Completing the word "Artisans" from the previous page's cutoff.</note>, are in the habit of supplying large <term>Households</term> <note>original: "Maisons." Here referring to the grand estates and domestic establishments of the nobility.</note> in all branches of their expenditure. These <term>Entrepreneurs</term> <note>In Cantillon's work, an "Entrepreneur" is anyone who operates at a risk, such as a merchant or master craftsman who buys at a certain price to sell at an uncertain one.</note> almost always need to borrow money to supply them: and when the <term>Lords</term> <note>original: "Seigneurs."</note> consume their revenues in advance and borrow money, they contribute doubly to raising the price of interest.

In the 18th century, this word typically referred to thriftiness or the careful management of a private household's resources.</note>, and buy first-hand as much as they can, they have many things procured by their <term>Servants</term> <note>original: "Valets."</note> without these things passing through the hands of Entrepreneurs; they thus decrease the profits and the number of Entrepreneurs in the State, and consequently the number of <term>Borrowers</term>, and furthermore the price of interest, because these types of Entrepreneurs, working on their own funds, do not [borrow]... <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break.</note>

<summary>The author explains that interest rates rise when the nobility spends excessively and relies on credit-dependent middlemen, whereas frugality and direct purchasing by the wealthy reduce the demand for loans and lower interest rates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Richard Cantillon’s explanation of how international trade balances are settled. On the previous page, he established a scenario where Paris imports lace from Brussels. Here, he explains the mechanism of "compensation"—how bills of exchange allow merchants to settle debts without the risky and expensive process of physically shipping silver between cities.</meta>

<header>->Essay on the Nature [of Trade]<-</header>

through the agency of the **Entrepreneurs** <term>Entrepreneur</term> <note>Cantillon defines the entrepreneur as a strategic risk-taker who facilitates the movement of goods between producers and consumers.</note> & the **Bankers** who will involve themselves on both sides.
The **Ladies of Paris** will pay one hundred thousand ounces of silver to whoever sells and delivers the **lace** to them; this seller will then pay that sum to the **Banker**, who will provide him with one or several **bills of exchange** <term>Bill of Exchange</term> <note>A written order (similar to a modern check but for international trade) that allowed a merchant to pay a debt in one city by using credit they held in another.</note> drawn on his correspondent in **Brussels**.
This Banker will then hand over the money to the **Champagne wine Merchants** who are owed 100,000 ounces of silver in **Brussels**, and who will give him their bills of exchange for the same value, drawn upon him by his Correspondent in **Brussels**.
In this way, the 100,000 ounces paid for the **Champagne wine** in **Brussels** will compensate <note>original: "compenſeront" — the process of offsetting mutual debts so that only the remaining balance needs to be settled in cash.</note> for the 100,000 ounces paid for the **lace** in **Paris**; by which means, one will save the trouble of transporting <note>original: "voiturer" — literally to carry by carriage; a costly and dangerous endeavor when moving large amounts of precious metal.</note> the silver received in **Paris** all the way to **Brussels**, and the trouble of transporting the silver received in **Brussels** all the way to

<summary>Cantillon describes the role of bankers and bills of exchange in international trade, showing how debts for different goods (lace and wine) can cancel each other out. This "compensation" mechanism avoids the physical transport of silver, reducing costs and risks for merchants in Paris and Brussels.</summary>

<keywords>bills of exchange, trade balance, silver transport, clearing debts, bankers, entrepreneurs, luxury trade, Paris, Brussels</keywords>

in gold, or failing gold, in silver. He will pay ten **livres** for each bag of one thousand **livres**, or in the language of Bankers, one percent; he will pay his Correspondent in Châlons five **livres** per bag of one thousand **livres** as a commission, or half a percent, and he will keep one percent for his own profit. At this rate, the exchange in Paris for Châlons is at two and a half percent above <term>par</term> <note>original: "au-dessus du pair." This indicates the cost of the exchange is higher than the intrinsic value of the money, reflecting the added expenses of transport and service fees.</note>, because one pays two **livres** ten **sols** on every hundred **livres** for the price of the exchange.

It is in this manner, roughly speaking, that the balance of trade is transferred from one city to another through the mediation of Bankers, and usually in large sums. Not everyone who holds the title of Banker is engaged in this practice; there are many who concern themselves only with commissions and <term>banking speculation</term> <note>original: "spéculation de banque." ## CHAPTER III.

### *Further Clarifications for Understanding the Nature of Exchange.*

<margin><unclear>symbol</unclear></margin>
We have seen that exchange rates are regulated based on the intrinsic value of the **specie** <note>Physical gold or silver coins.</note>—that is, based on the **par**—and that their variation stems from the costs and risks of transportation from one place to another, when the **balance of trade** <term>The net difference in value between a country's imports and exports.</term> must be sent in hard currency. No complex reasoning is needed for a matter that is so evident in actual fact and practice. Bankers sometimes introduce certain refinements into this practice.

If England owes France one hundred thousand ounces of silver for

### Essai sur la nature ###

of the <term>minting of coins</term><note>original: "fabrication des eſpeces"; the process of converting raw bullion into legal tender, which included a fee known as seigniorage</note>; which usually amounts to one and a half percent, that is to say, the price of the coins is always set above that of the raw <term>bullion</term><note>original: "matieres"; gold or silver in its raw, uncoined form, such as bars or ingots</note>. Although the <term>exchange rates</term><note>original: "prix des changes"; the rate at which one currency is exchanged for another</note> hardly vary except in relation to the balance of trade between the State and other countries, and although this balance is naturally nothing but the difference in the value of the commodities and merchan-

### Essay on the Nature of Trade

annually from such <term>funds</term><note>original: "fonds"; referring here to the public or private investments mentioned on the previous page</note> etc. Exchange rates do not fail to vary according to all these accidental causes, and to follow the rule of the <term>transport of money</term><note>original: "transport d'argent"; the physical shipping of gold or silver coins to settle debts when paper exchange is unavailable</note> which is required; and in the consideration of the <term>balance of trade</term><note>original: "balance du commerce"; the net difference between a country's exports and imports</note>, one does not separate—and indeed, one would find it difficult to separate—these types of items; they most certainly influence the increase and decrease of a <term>State's</term><note>original: "Etat"; the national or sovereign entity</note> actual money, and its comparative strength and power.

My subject does not allow me to expand upon the effects of these accidental causes; I shall always limit myself to simple views of commerce, for fear of cluttering my subject, which is already cluttered enough by the multiplicity of facts presented here.

### Of Trade. Part III.

to the English Ships that are in the Lisbon **Roadstead**<note>original: "Rade"; a sheltered area of water outside a harbor where ships can anchor</note>, even at the risk of their lives. They will initially earn a hundred or fifty percent from this business, and this profit is paid by the Portuguese inhabitants through the high price they give for the **cloth**<note>original: "drap"; specifically woolen cloth, a staple of English exports to Portugal</note>. They will gradually become familiar with this practice, after having performed it often with success, and in time we will see money carried on board the English Ships for a fee of two or one percent.

The King of Portugal makes the law or the prohibition: his Subjects, even his Courtiers, pay the costs of the risk run to make the prohibition useless, and to evade it. No advantage is therefore derived from such a law; on the contrary, it causes a real disadvantage to Portugal because it is the reason that more money leaves the State than would leave if no such law existed.

### Essay on the Nature [of Commerce]

For those who profit from this **scheme**<note>original: "manége"; refers here to the clever or underhanded methods used to evade trade laws</note>, whether they be Jews<note>In the 18th century, Jewish merchant networks were central to international finance, though they often faced legal restrictions and the threat of persecution, which made their capital highly mobile</note> or others, they do not fail to send their profits to foreign countries; and when they have acquired enough, or when fear takes hold of them, they often follow their money themselves.

Even if some of these offenders were caught in the act, if their property were confiscated and they were put to death, this circumstance and this execution, instead of preventing the outflow of money, would only increase it. This is because those who were previously satisfied with one or two percent for smuggling money out would then demand twenty or fifty percent; thus, enough must always leave to pay the **balance**<note>original: "balance"; referring to the "balance of trade." If a country imports more than it exports, it must eventually pay the difference in hard currency, regardless of prohibitions</note>.

->384 OF TRADE. PART III.<-

foreign countries, they have considerable quantities of them brought in for several years' consumption: all this makes money circulate with greater velocity, all this raises the prices of all things; these high prices prevent the foreigner from drawing goods from France as usual: France keeps its own goods, and at the same time draws in large quantities of goods from abroad. France is everywhere the **dupe** <note>A victim of a bad deal or deception.</note> of these operations; she pays very high prices for foreign goods during **diminutions** <term>diminution</term> <note>A policy where the government lowers the face value of coins.</note>, and she resells them at a bargain price during the **augmentation** <term>augmentation</term> <note>A policy where the government raises the face value of coins.</note> to those same Foreigners. She sells her own goods at a bargain price, which she had held at such a high price during the diminutions; thus, it would be difficult for all the **specie** <note>French: *eſpeces*; physical gold or silver coins.</note> that left France during the diminutions to return during the augmentation.

If the coins of the **new minting** <note>French: *nouvelle fabrique*.</note> are counterfeited abroad, as almost always happens, France loses the twenty percent that the King established as the **mint tax** <note>Also known as seigniorage; the fee the monarch takes for converting bullion into legal tender.</note>; this is just as much gained for the Foreigner, who profits furthermore from the low price of goods in France.

# Essay on the Nature [of Commerce]

->390<-

The King makes a **considerable profit** from the **mint tax** <note>French: *taxe de la monnoie*, also known as seigniorage; the fee taken by the monarch for converting bullion into legal tender.</note>, but it costs France three times as much to provide him with this profit.

It is easy to understand that during times when there is a favorable **balance of trade** <note>The difference between the value of a country's exports and its imports.</note> for France against Foreigners, the King is able to draw a tax of twenty percent or more through a **new minting** <note>French: *nouvelle fabrication*.</note> of **specie** <note>French: *especes*; physical gold or silver coins.</note> and by an **augmentation** <term>augmentation</term> <note>Raising the nominal value of the coins (e.g., declaring that a coin previously worth 4 livres is now worth 5).</note> of their **nominal value** <note>French: *valeur numéraire*; the value stamped on the coin as opposed to its intrinsic metal weight.</note>.

But if the balance of trade were against France at the time of this new minting and augmentation, it would not be successful, and the King would not derive a great profit from it. The reason is that, in these circumstances, one is constantly obliged to send money abroad to the Foreigner. Now, the **old crown** <note>French: *écu vieux*; the version of the coin used before the new minting was ordered.</note> is worth just as much in foreign countries as the crown from the new minting. This being so, the

### of Commerce. PART III.

Jews and Bankers will offer a premium or profit privately <note>French: *entre quatre yeux*, literally "between four eyes"; referring to informal or secret transactions outside of official channels.</note> for the old coins <note>French: *vieilles especes*; the coins whose nominal value is about to be or has been officially changed.</note>, and the private individual who can sell them above the price offered by the Mint <note>French: *la Monnoie*; the state institution responsible for coining and regulating currency.</note> will not take them there. At the Mint, he is only given about four livres for his **crown** <note>French: *écu*; a large silver coin. Cantillon is illustrating how the official rate might be lower than the "street" rate during a currency transition.</note>, but the Banker will first give him four livres five sols <note>The *livre* was the primary unit of account, divided into 20 *sols* (or *sous*).</note>, then four livres ten, and finally four livres fifteen. Such a failure can hardly happen when the **augmentation** is carried out after scheduled **devaluations** <term>diminutions</term> <note>French: *diminutions*; the practice of officially lowering the nominal value of coins, often used to encourage people to bring their coins to the Mint to be exchanged before they lose even more value.</note>, because then the balance of trade naturally turns in favor of France, in the manner we have explained.

# Essay on the Nature [of Commerce]

# <page-num>402</page-num>
## Essay on the Nature [of Commerce]

(the majority) <note>Completing the thought from the previous page: "several Bankers of this kind, (the majority)..."</note> always keep in their cash reserve <term>caisse</term> <note>The physical vault or chest where liquid specie was kept.</note> half of the sums deposited with them, and lend the other half at interest and put it into circulation. In this second example, the Banker circulates his notes <term>billets</term> <note>Promissory notes or early banknotes representing a claim on silver.</note> for one hundred thousand ounces or crowns <term>écus</term> <note>The écu was a large silver coin used in 18th-century France.</note> with only fifty thousand crowns in hand.

If he has a large stream of deposits and great credit, this increases the confidence people have in his notes and makes them less eager to demand payment; but this only delays his payments by a few days or weeks when the notes fall into the hands of people who are not accustomed to using his services, and he must always govern his actions by the behavior of those who are in the habit of entrusting him with their money: if his notes fall into the hands of those of his own trade <note>Meaning other bankers or financial competitors.</note>, they will be in a great hurry to withdraw the money.

->**Vocabulary**<-

A **national Bank** in the Capital of a great Kingdom or State seems destined to contribute less to the utility of circulation—owing to the distance of its Provinces—than in a small State; and when money circulates there in greater abundance than among its Neighbors, a national Bank does more harm than good. An abundance of **fictitious and imaginary money** <note>original: "argent fictif & imaginaire"; Cantillon refers here to bank credits and paper notes that increase the money supply without a corresponding increase in physical gold or silver.</note> causes the same disadvantages as an increase of real money in circulation, by raising the price of land and labor, and by increasing the cost of goods and **Manufactures** at the risk of losing them <note>Losing the market for these goods to foreign competitors who can produce them more cheaply.</note> in the future: but this **stealthy abundance** <note>original: "abondance furtive"; an increase in the money supply that is not immediately visible because it is based on credit rather than metal.</note> vanishes at the first puff of discredit, and precipitates disorder.

->of Commerce. PART III.<-

the payments of eighty million.

**Bankers** in **Paris** have often remarked that the same bag of money has returned to them four or five times in the payments of a single day, when they had much to pay and to receive.

I believe **public Banks** to be of very great utility in small **States**, and in those where money is somewhat scarce; but I believe them to be of little use for the solid advantage of a large **Kingdom**.

The Emperor **Tiberius** <note>Roman Emperor from 14 to 37 AD, known for his administrative focus and fiscal restraint.</note>, a stern and thrifty Prince, had amassed in the **Treasury of the Empire** two billion seven hundred million **Sesterces** <note>The sesterce was a silver, and later bronze, coin of Ancient Rome.</note>, which corresponds to twenty-five million **pounds sterling**, or one hundred million ounces of silver: an immense sum in **specie** <term>specie: money in the form of physical coins (gold or silver) rather than paper credit.</term> for those times, and even for today. ### Essay on the Nature of Commerce

ey, he hindered circulation, and money became much rarer in **Rome** than it had ever been.

**Tiberius**, who attributed this scarcity to the monopolies of the **Businessmen** and **Financiers** <note>These "Financiers" or "Gens d'affaires" were often tax farmers—private individuals who contracted with the state to collect taxes in exchange for a fee or a portion of the revenue.</note> who farmed the revenues of the **Empire**, ordered by an **Edict** that they should purchase land for at least two-thirds of their capital. This **Edict**, instead of stimulating circulation, threw it into complete disorder: all the **Financiers** tightened and recalled their funds <note>This refers to a "credit crunch," where lenders stop providing loans and demand immediate repayment of existing debts.</note>, under the pretext of putting themselves in a position to obey the **Edict** by buying land. Consequently, the land, instead of rising in value, fell to a much lower price due to the scarcity of money in circulation. **Tiberius** remedied this scarcity of money by lending to private individuals upon good security <note>original: "sous bonnes cautions"</note> only three hundred million **Sesterces**: that is to say, the ninth part of the **specie** <term>specie: physical metal coins (gold or silver) as opposed to paper credit or bills of exchange.</term> that he had in his **treasury**.

->Of Commerce. PART III.<-

If the ninth part of the treasury was sufficient in **Rome** to restore circulation, it would seem that the establishment of a **general Bank** <note>This likely refers to the concept of a national or central bank, such as the Bank of England or the "Banque Générale" established by John Law in France in 1716.</note> in a large **Kingdom**, where its utility would never even correspond to a tenth of the money in circulation (provided money is not being hoarded), would offer no real or permanent advantage. When considered for its intrinsic value, such a bank can only be seen as an expedient to gain time.

### Essay on the Nature [of Trade].

Experience in the buying and selling of capital from public funds clearly shows that the sums involved are considerable; without these purchases and sales, the amounts deposited at the Bank would undoubtedly be less substantial.

This means that when a State is not in debt and has no need for the buying and selling of capital, the assistance of a Bank will be less necessary and less significant there.

In the year 1720, the capital of public funds and of the **Bubbles** <note>original: "Bublles." This refers to the South Sea Bubble in England and similar speculative manias where stock prices were inflated far beyond their value before collapsing.</note>—which were scams and enterprises of private companies in London—amounted to a value of eight hundred million pounds sterling. Nevertheless, the buying and selling of such "poisonous" capital was carried out without difficulty due to the quantity of **banknotes** <term>banknotes: paper credit instruments issued by a bank that circulate as money</term> of all kinds that were put into the market, so long as people were satisfied with these same papers.

### On Commerce. Part III.

In the 18th century, a carriage and team of horses were the ultimate status symbols of the wealthy.</note>, foreign linens, and silks—hard currency <term>hard currency: known as "specie," these are coins minted from precious metals like gold or silver</term> was required for all of it. I mean this in regard to the spending of interest earned, and this demand for cash tore all the financial systems to pieces.

This example clearly shows that paper money and the credit of public and private banks can produce surprising effects in everything that does not involve ordinary spending on food and drink, clothing, and other household necessities. However, in the steady and uniform flow of circulation, the assistance of banks and of credit of this kind is far less significant and less solid than is generally thought. Money <note>original: "L'argent." While this is the modern French word for money, in this historical context it specifically refers to silver or metallic currency.</note> alone is the true sinew of circulation.

## CHAPTER VIII.

*On the Refinements of the Credit of General Banks.*

The national Bank of London <note>The author refers here to the Bank of England, which was established in 1694 and became the model for modern central banking.</note> is composed of a great number of Shareholders who choose Directors to manage its operations. Their primary advantage consisted of an annual distribution of the profits made through the interest on the money lent out from the funds deposited at the Bank; subsequently, public funds <term>public funds: government debt or national stocks that the bank managed in exchange for interest payments from the state</term> were incorporated into it, for which the State pays an annual interest.

Despite such a solid foundation, it has been seen (when the Bank had made large advances <note>original: "avances." In this context, these are loans or credits extended to the government, often to fund wars or administrative costs.</note> to the State, and the holders of bank notes <term>bank notes: at this time, these were paper promises to pay the bearer the equivalent value in gold or silver coin upon request</term> feared that the Bank would be embarrassed—

If the Bank alone raises the price of public funds by buying them, it would lower them by just as much when it wishes to resell them in order to cancel its extraordinary notes; however, it always happens that many private individuals, wishing to imitate the Bank Agents in their operations, contribute to sustaining these prices; there are even some who are caught out <note>original: "attrapés," meaning tricked or trapped by the market's artificial movements</note> for lack of knowing the truth of these operations, which involve an infinity of refinements, or rather <term>deceits: Cantillon uses the word "fourberies," implying dishonest schemes or trickery in financial dealings</term>, which are not my subject.

# on Commerce. PART III. #

429

It is therefore established that a Bank in collusion with a Minister is capable of raising and sustaining the price of public funds, and of lowering the rate of interest within the State at the pleasure of that Minister, when the operations are managed with discretion, and thereby clearing the debts of the State; but these <term>refinements: sophisticated financial maneuvers that Cantillon often equates with clever but deceptive tricks</term> which open the door to making great fortunes, are very rarely managed for the sole benefit of the State; and the operators are most often corrupted by them. The <term>extraordinary bank notes: currency issued specifically for these financial maneuvers, rather than being backed by the bank's usual reserves of gold or silver</term>, which are manufactured and distributed on these occasions, do not disturb the general circulation, because, being used for the purchase and sale of capital assets, they are not used for household spending, and are not converted into <note>original: "argent," referring here to specie or hard coin</note> money; but if some fear or unforeseen accident drove the

It marks a transition from the foundational socio-economic principles discussed in Part One—such as land ownership and population—to more technical explorations of monetary theory, including barter, market dynamics, and the velocity of money.</meta>

### TABLE OF THE CHAPTERS.
---
## SECOND PART.

**CHAP. I.** *On Barter.* <note>original: "Du Troc." The direct exchange of goods or services without using money as an intermediary.</note> 151

**CHAP. II.** *On Market Prices.* 155

**CHAP. III.** *On the Circulation of Money.* 159

**CHAP. IV.** *Further reflections on the speed or slowness of the circulation of money in barter.* 183

**CHAP. V.** *On the inequality of the circulation of hard cash* <term>hard cash: translated from "argent effectif," this refers to physical currency or specie (coins) actually moving through the economy.</term> *in a State.* 197

**CHAP. VI.** *On the increase and decrease of the quantity of hard cash in a State.* 211

**CHAP. VII.** *Continuation of the same subject on the increase and decrease of the quantity of hard cash in a State.* 232

<summary>This page presents the table of contents for the second part of the work, focusing on the mechanics of barter, market pricing, and the factors influencing the flow and volume of physical money within a nation's economy.</summary>
<keywords>barter, market prices, circulation of money, monetary velocity, hard cash, specie, Richard Cantillon, economic theory</keywords>

## THIRD PART.

**CHAP. I.** ON *Foreign*
*Trade.* <note>original: "Commerce avec l'Etranger." This marks the shift from domestic economic theory to international markets.</note> *297*

**CHAP. II.** *On Foreign Exchange* <term>foreign exchange: translated from "changes," referring to the system by which the commercial nations of the world discharge their debts to each other.</term> *&*
*its nature. 323*

**CHAP. III.** *Further clarifica-*
*tions for the understanding of*
*the nature of foreign exchange. 340*

**CHAP. IV.** *On the variations of the*
*proportion of values, with*
*respect to the Metals which serve*
*as money. 355*

<note>A faint circular library stamp is visible over the text for Chapters III and IV.</note>

<note>The following technical terms are central to these chapters: hard cash (argent effectif), interest of money (interêt de l'argent), foreign trade (commerce avec l'étranger), exchange (changes), metals (métaux), and money/currency (monnoie).</note>

<summary>This page concludes the table of contents for Part Two, focusing on interest rates and cash volume, and introduces Part Three, which covers international trade, foreign exchange, and the relative value of monetary metals.</summary>
<keywords>interest rates, hard cash, specie, foreign trade, foreign exchange, monetary metals, Richard Cantillon, economic theory</keywords>

# TABLE OF THE CHAPTERS.

**Chap. V.** *On the increase & the decrease of the value of denominated currency.* <term>denominated currency: translated from "especes monnoïées en dénomination," this refers to the face value assigned to coins by the state, which could be legally changed regardless of the actual metal content.</term> 381

**Chap. VI.** *On Banks, & their credit.* 397

**Chap. VII.** *Further clarifications & inquiries into the utility of a National Bank.* 406

**Chap. VIII.** *On the refinements of credit in General Banks.* <note>original: "Banques générales." This likely refers to large-scale public or central banking institutions that manage broader economic credit beyond simple private deposits.</note> 426.
